---
title: Human Palette (extended) palette
date: 2023-03-18
---
The densified canonical shade set (#112 decision 9): the 25 human-palette anchors fixed plus 99 Lloyd-relaxed extras on the Tartan Dictionary fine grid, each named as a roman tone of its anchor (base = SplitTone(code)). Generated by cmd/shadefill (research/colour-shade-fill); the quantiser snaps here first, then takes the shade's human anchor - two short hops instead of one unbounded one..

Print it: [**the Human Palette (extended) shade card (PDF)**](/colour/human-extended/human-extended-shades.pdf) — every shade as a 24 mm swatch with its code, name and the reference values to dye against.

> **These are not named colours, and not a palette to design in.** The extended set is the quantiser's *internal* densification of the human palette — indicative in-between shades, spaced on the fine grid, whose only job is to tell two near-identical dyes apart when one sett carries both. Nothing is *named* here: a colour you pick still reads as its nearest human-palette anchor (**B**, never an in-between), the extra only taken as a roman tone (**Bi**, **Bii**…).
>
> Treat a shade here as **a way of expressing a variant or sett that has no colour palette of its own** — nothing more. A sett or variant that genuinely needs these shades to be told apart should be recorded as a **variant**, not pinned to an extended-palette code.

And plotted over the fine grid in three illuminance layers — light, mid and dark. Each wheel is one lightness layer plotted in OKLab's own dimensions: the angle is hue — red at the top, then clockwise through orange and brown, yellow, green, blue and purple, the wheel's jog order — and the radius is chroma, with rings every 0.05. The faint grey dots are the fine grid at that lightness; the red dashed line is the dyeable limit. Hover a dot for its code, name and hex. The small dots are the human palette, the full dots this palette's own shades — so you can read how it lands among the named colours.

## The shades

Each shade in OKLCh — lightness **L**, chroma **C** and hue **h** (degrees). The small number under each is that axis's fine-grid index: the lattice level, chroma ring and snapped hue angle that together make the shade's grid code.
